*The original rung-8 record follows, kept for its probed facts.*


**The ruling.** *"Eventually we're going to have to account for the situation where one visit is
assigned two or more technicians, and that should count as one instead of two. But I agree that we
should finish build 6 and 7 before getting into this. But I want both builds to keep in mind of
this, and perhaps have the plumbing pre-built for this. Put this issue at the tail of the build
order."* So: **tail of the order, after 7 — but 6 and 7 must not foreclose it.**

**What is already true, probed 2026-09-01 (do not re-derive this).** The storage and the write
path ALREADY do multi-person; only the reading, counting and asking layers assume one person.

- `assignments` is `(job, technician, time window)`. `no_confirmed_overlap` is keyed
  `technician_id WITH =`, so it blocks one PERSON double-booked and never two people sharing a
  slot. Probed on the local stack: two technicians + a seat-exempt helper, same job, same
  09:00–12:00, all three `confirmed`; the same technician twice correctly refused.
- Through the real tool path: a manager's agent books each person with its own `book_visit` call
  naming the same `jobId`. `resolveBooking`'s only person-check is that technician's own overlap.
  The code even names the escape hatch — *"if two same-window visits for one customer are truly
  intended, stagger the time or attach technicians"* (`booking.ts`).
- **A technician's seat agent has NO scheduling verb at any tier.** `book_visit` is in
  `ADMIN_TOOLS`, gated at tools/list AND at dispatch (`ADMIN_TOOL_NAMES.has(name) &&
  !activeManager`). Only managers (or their agents) can put a second person on a job.
- **Live specimen in the pilot** (booked by the user 2026-09-01, kept as the reference case):
  job `f8240fc1` "Water heater flush" / Henderson Family, 2026-09-02 2:00–3:30pm firm-local, Ben
  Shelton (technician) + Joy (manager) — two `confirmed` rows, two `book_visit` calls 1.7s apart.
  Tier is no barrier to being BOOKED; it gates only who may CALL the tool.
- `visits` — the L2 table with the single `technician_name` TEXT column — is **dormant**:
  `createVisit` has no production caller. It is a decoy when reasoning about this, not the
  constraint. Whether it should be retired outright is open.

**Where the one-person assumption actually lives** — these are build 8's scope:

- **Counting.** `get_team_schedule` returns the pilot specimen as `counts.total = 2`, two stops.
  "How many visits tomorrow?" answers two for one visit.
- **Detection.** `listOverdueConfirmedAssignments` returns assignment ROWS with no tier filter and
  no same-visit collapse, and `subject_ref` is the assignment id *(⚠ since rung 9b it is
  `${visitId}@${starts_at}` — `src/jobs/nudgeKinds.ts`; the id half is now stable across moves)* —
  so one two-person visit mints TWO gaps addressed to two people. The group-send rail then correctly refuses to merge them (one
  message goes to one chat), so both get texted about the same visit.
- **Booking.** No verb takes several people; the manager calls `book_visit` once per person and
  the confirmation says nothing about who else is already on that slot.

**What already mitigates it, partially (keep these — they are the accidental crew handling).**
The freshness test is JOB-scoped (`hasActivitySince(job_id, starts_at)`), so if EITHER person
files a note before the tick, NEITHER gap is minted. And when two gaps do share a job the planner
gets the shared-evidence warning ("everything below is SHARED between them"). What is missing is
any statement that the two people were at the SAME visit — today that reads identically to a
plumber and an electrician at one address on unrelated tasks.

**The plumbing 6 and 7 must leave open (the user's "pre-built" ask).** Not yet designed —
candidates recorded so the two builds can choose deliberately rather than by accident:

- **Build 6 (vocabulary):** the verbs must not bake in one-person-per-gap. `escalate =
  ask(manager)` (ruled 2026-09-01) is already a message, so it is safe. The live question is
  whether an ask names a PERSON or a VISIT — an intent that can say "ask whoever is likeliest to
  know, not both" is the multi-tech-safe shape, and it is a judgment, which is exactly what build
  6 hands to the planner.
- **Build 7 (detectors report):** the load-bearing seam. Build 7 decides what a gap's SUBJECT is;
  "one gap per assignment row" vs "one gap per visit" is that decision. ⚠ **Half of this is now
  ANSWERED by rung 9b (2026-09-05) — do not re-derive it when planning 9c.** The old text read:
  *"There is no id today that names a visit across its rows — the options are a `visit_group_id`
  column on `assignments` or a derived key (`job_id` + window)."* **There is such an id now:** a
  move is an UPDATE in place, so `visits.id` names the trip for its whole life. **Do not build
  `visit_group_id`.** What 9b did NOT solve is the crew: `technician_id` is still a single nullable
  column, so two people on one trip still means two rows with two ids, and 9c's participant table
  is what collapses them. Note also that the once-ever dedupe now keys on `${visitId}@${starts_at}`,
  not the bare id — a 9c subject must stay compatible with `visitSubjectCandidates()`.
- **SETTLED 2026-09-01 (user): a crew has NO LEAD.** Everyone on a visit is equal — do not build a
  lead/helper distinction, a primary-technician column, or an "answered on behalf of" role. The
  consequence to hold: one person's note satisfies the visit for EVERYONE on it, which is already
  how the job-scoped freshness test behaves, so this ruling RATIFIES the existing accident rather
  than asking for new machinery. Attribution (`answered_by_capture_id`) still records WHO spoke —
  that is provenance, not rank, and it stays.

## 6. The build log — the intelligent-spine ladder (2026-08-22 → 2026-08-24)

The ladder's own record, oldest first, kept at full detail because the reasoning is what a later
session needs. **Section 4 says what is true today; this section says how it got there.** Each
entry is a `###` heading stamped with its date — grep the date or the stage number (①–⑤).

### The build order, revised (2026-08-22)

**Build order, revised 2026-08-22 (third revision).** Two user rulings reshaped it.

*Ruling 1 — closing is the planner's job.* The sweep runs every 5 min and the planner every 30, so
the fast deterministic thing always won the race, which is backwards from the AI-manager premise.
Only `filled` ("did this answer the question?") is a judgment; `moot`, escalate, expire and the
delivery caps are facts. `filled` therefore stops closing and the planner's `close` becomes the
normal end of a gap. Three things fall out free: two-gaps-one-job becomes judgeable (the gap stays
open, so the planner sees the answer), the planner gains a feedback loop it never had, and
`record_filled` is redundant because `basis:'record'` vs `basis:'evidence'` already carries the
answered-vs-filled distinction. Fail-safe = **one working day**, anchored to the existing
escalation deadline rather than a new constant (already 24h default, planner-adjustable, 48h cap,
and sitting exactly where a false escalation would land); it fires only when no APPLIED planner
*judgment* exists in 24h. **Cross-phase hazard:** the continuity phase writes `unmentioned` rows
with `outcome:'applied'`, so a naive liveness test would be true for every gap on every tick and
the fail-safe would silently never fire — count only real verbs.

*Ruling 2 — the planner should read basically everything.* **RESOLVED 2026-08-23 (④, plan
`thoughts/shared/plans/2026-08-23-the-brain-can-look.md`):** `v_captures` (labeled retraction
trail, sensitivity unfiltered per the same-day ruling), `v_visits` (the note→job bridge — a note's
own `job_id` is NULL by design, the durable link is the visit), `v_nudges` and `v_review_items`
joined the surface: twelve views. *(⚠ rung 9b, 2026-09-05: `v_visits` is DROPPED and replaced by
`v_visit_notes`, which walks the same bridge through the `visit_notes` link table. Still twelve.)* `nudge_decisions` stays deliberately view-less (the catalog is
shared with the manager's `query_spine`). Historical statement of the gap, kept for the reasoning:
`spine_read` exposed 8 views and **none covered `raw_captures`, `nudges` or `review_items`** — the
planner could not read what anyone said. This is why the read surface moved IN FRONT of the memory
work: the memory design is "push the keys, pull the content", and the pull half does not work if
the content is unreachable.

The board as it stood that day — **every line of it has since shipped; kept to show the order
things were decided in, not as status.** Outcome in brackets:

```
① eyes see the whole record   money trio                    MERGED #162
② the mouth stops lying       manager copy + one-shot notes  MERGED #163 (+ ②b)
③ THE BRAIN DECIDES           attribution · closing → planner · continuity   (plan written)
                                                            [MERGED #164, 2026-08-22]
④ THE BRAIN CAN LOOK          read surface: captures / nudges / review_items
                                                            [MERGED #167, 2026-08-22]
⑤ memory architecture         needs ③'s corpus AND ④'s reach
                                                            [MERGED #175/#176, 2026-08-23]
⑥ fixture-spine agentic eval at scale                       [MERGED #178, 2026-08-24]
── delivery costs nothing      `openclaw message send`; box-side, own re-wire. Independent
                               [④b, MERGED #171/#172/#174, 2026-08-23]
── Group B "the bucket drains" 90 open items, zero ever resolved. Independent
                               [STILL OPEN — 161 items on 2026-08-24, still zero resolved]
```

**Dead:** the gap-id stamp. A ref cannot survive the path — under `--deliver` it must pass through
an agent whose footer tells it to strip "any internal detail" (a stripped ref is silent), and under
direct send the technician would read it in their chat. Attribution is spine-side and code-only
now: at `record_field_note` time the spine knows the technician, resolves the job, and reads its
own open gaps — exactly one → stamp, two or more → let the planner judge.

### PR #163 deployed, fleet re-wired, and two recorded facts corrected (2026-08-22)

**The wire that plan ② owed.** PR #163 (`590998c`) shipped the one-shot `record_field_note`, and
until the seats were re-wired every one of them was serving the OLD two-step doctrine against a
one-shot spine — verified before touching anything: all six (s2, s3, s5, s6, s7, s8) matched
"STALE (two-step)". The `## Permanent-record writes` block is delete-then-append, so `wire`
genuinely refreshes it. After: all six "NEW", **exactly one block each** (no duplication),
containers untouched (`wire` restarts the gateway inside, not the container). Order followed:
s2 canary → verify in-container → fleet → audit a NON-s2 seat (s7, oldest uptime): all eight
`EXPECTED_SECTIONS` present, `toolSearch: false`, weekday guard loaded.

**Deploy verification is NOT optional here — the spine has `autoDeploy: "no"`.** Merging ships
nothing. `srv-d8uqmdegvqtc73bfn83g` went live on `590998c` at 18:57Z by a **manual** trigger;
the `lifecycle-heart` cron (`crn-d93iq7a8qa3s73ap6260`, autoDeploy yes) went live at 18:35Z.
Check both — the cron and the web service are separate deploys of the same commit.

**CORRECTION 1 — the bootstrap-copy ranking is not stable.** There are **six** copies on the
box, not four, and the Aug-20 note naming `~/bootstrap-box.sh` as the stalest was already wrong
by Aug-22 (it had become mid-pack at `973afe2f`; the stalest were `~/deploy-recycler`, Jul-12,
and `~/Box_Setup_Stuff`, Aug-15). On 2026-08-22 **no copy matched the repo at all.** The durable
rule is md5-against-the-repo every time; any recorded table of which copy is stale is a snapshot
that rots. Fixed by `rsync`ing the repo tree to `~/Box_Setup_Stuff/` and verifying the hash
before running anything from it.

**CORRECTION 2 — the "fleet parked on deepinfra-prod" note is RETIRED as wrong.** Verified live
2026-08-22 20:02Z: the installed medic is md5 `0921ab1e` (the repo-identical **Fireworks** build
— the DeepInfra hand-edit is gone), its timer is `active`, and its tick reports *"route OK on
fireworks-prod / accounts/fireworks/routers/glm-5p2-fast — serving verified (tool call
returned)"*. The monthly spend limit was raised. A future session acting on the parked note
would have reverted a healthy fleet. DeepInfra stays configured as the manual rollback.

**Nudge delivery, measured from real trajectories (not flag names).** `--deliver` does not create
a session — `--session-id` does, and `nudge-runner.sh` pins a fresh `nudge-<uuid>` per nudge. The
nudge session is **one-way**: the spine injects the question as a `user` turn, the agent replies to
Telegram, and the technician's answer routes to their **normal** session (proof on s2: nudge
`fd98ab15` delivered into `nudge-fd98ab15…` 23:17, Sam's "all done" landed in `8dc26855…` 23:18;
`sessions_list` inside the nudge session returns `count: 0`). **Any design that passes a token out
and expects the nudge agent to hand it back is dead.**

**But the channel is shared context.** openclaw injects a rolling window of recent CHANNEL messages
into every turn as `Conversation context (untrusted, chronological, selected for current message)`,
whatever session produced them — Sam's main session carried `#761`–`#771` across three days with
the nudge's delivered text immediately before his reply. Combined with **`openclaw message send`**
(present on the deployed 2026.6.34, no inference — docs.openclaw.ai/cli/message), **delivery can
cost zero inference.** There is no documented way to append to a session transcript without
inference, and none is needed.

Today one nudge costs **6 turns, 84,251 uncached input + 156,608 cacheRead + 3,245 output**, and
the turns are the agent *searching for ids the message never gave it* — `memory_search`,
`find_customer`, `list_recent_captures`, `sessions_list`, `get_customer_context`, `list_team`.
`assembleNudgeMessage` prints a customer NAME and job TITLE and no ids: the repo's own "you cannot
prompt your way to a UUID" finding, fixed for the planner and never for the seat.

Four things a direct-send design must handle: (1) the **duty** does not cross the channel
(`record-keeping check` scored 0 hits in the main session — the footer is inbound-only, so the
instruction to call `record_field_note` must move into always-loaded AGENTS.md doctrine); (2) the
context window is **bounded** (~11 messages / 3 days observed); (3) "selected for current message"
implies selection logic, n=1, recency-vs-relevance unverified; (4) delivery verification currently
reads assistant text from the trajectory and would need the send's own result instead. Also found:
`memory_search` on s2 returns `{"disabled": true, "unavailable": true}` and is burned every turn,
and the footer's "First read back through this conversation" is unsatisfiable in a fresh session —
which is exactly why the agent reaches for `sessions_list`.

**Re-confirmed trap:** a non-interactive `ssh` has no `~/.local/bin` on PATH, so
`bootstrap-box.sh wire` dies at its bare `nemoclaw` call — `export PATH="$HOME/.local/bin:$PATH"`
first. Already recorded; hit again because the recorded fix is easy to skip.


### Deploy does not migrate — and the pilot's migration ledger lies (2026-08-23)

Found while deploying PR #164 (③ the brain decides). Two separate traps, one incident.

**Render deploys no migrations.** The web service builds with `npm install && npm run build` and
starts `npm run mcp:serve:http`; the cron runs `npm run job:lifecycle`. Nothing in either invokes
`supabase migration up` or `db push`. So the merge commit went live at 00:44Z against a pilot schema
that still lacked both of its migrations; the schema arrived ~12 minutes later, by hand, and only
because it was checked. **Deploy and migrate are two independent manual steps. Run the migration
FIRST, then the code** — the reverse is what happened here.

**The pilot's migration ledger is out of sync with its actual schema.** `list_migrations` stopped at
`nudge_decisions_extend`, yet `nudge_decisions.handoff_note` and `question_draft` (PR #163) were
present as columns. Some DDL reaches the pilot by a path Supabase does not record, so the ledger can
report a migration missing when it is applied — and would equally hide the reverse.

> **Verify the artefact, never the record of the artefact.** `information_schema.columns` and
> `pg_get_constraintdef(...)`, not `list_migrations`. Same doctrine as the six drifting
> `bootstrap-box.sh` copies on trades-box-1.

**Why the CHECK constraint was the dangerous half.** A missing nullable column is benign — Phase 1's
attribution no-ops into its own try/catch. A missing CHECK is not: `recordNudgeDecisions` inserts one
statement per chunk with no partial insert, so a single un-migrated value rejects **up to 500 rows**.
Phase 3 appends `unmentioned` for every gap the planner does not name, so *every* tick's batch would
have failed — and the tick still reports `ok: true`, because the write sits inside a try/catch. The
knock-on is the bad part: `plannerAlive` reads that table, so with nothing being written it reads
false for every gap, and Phase 2's fail-safe would auto-close answered gaps while blaming a planner
that was working perfectly.

**Verified-good state, 2026-08-23 01:01:17Z** (the first in-window planner tick after the fix):
`decisions 2 · dropped 0 · applied 2 · recorded 2 · tenantErrors 0`, `byAction {"leave": 2}`, both
carrying substantive reasons; `watchers.nudgesAutoClosed: 0`. `recorded: 2` is the proof the
constraint took — pre-migration that number is 0 with a loud error and no other symptom.

#### It bit again — a 23-hour silent outage, and AUTO-DEPLOY is why (2026-09-04 → 09-05)

The rule above was written down and still cost us a day, because nobody was at the keyboard.

**What happened.** PR #201 was a one-line CRLF fix. It merged to `main` at 01:29Z and Render
**auto-deployed** `48a9bd9` to `lifecycle-heart` at 01:33Z — carrying builds 4/5/5.5/6-era code onto
a pilot database missing **14 migrations**. A trivial doc-shaped PR shipped a schema-dependent
runtime. Last healthy tick `01:25:17Z` (`ok: true`, `tenantErrors: 0`); every tick after it
`ok: false`, until the schema was applied at ~00:30Z the next day.

**Cost: detection and the planner were dead for ~23 hours** (`planned: 0`, `tenantErrors: 1`) while
pairing, sessions, logIngest and costRollup all stayed green — so the tick looked *partly* alive,
which is the shape that hides things here (cf. the dead manager-push that ran 30 days).
`20260901130000_observations.sql` had predicted it in its own header: *"apply this migration BEFORE
deploying the code … a missing relation fails EVERY tenant's pass."*

**Recognise the signature.** `Could not find the table 'public.X' in the schema cache` is PostgREST,
and it nearly always means the relation was never created — not that a cache is stale. **Degrade-only
reads hide the true scale:** `job_notes`, `planner_memory` and `planner_runs` logged
`"… failed — running without"` and only `observations` threw, so the tick summary named ONE missing
table when four were missing. Grep the whole log for `schema cache`, never just the summary.

**The ledger is worse than "out of sync" — it is unusable for `db push`.** Its recorded versions do
not correspond to this repo's filenames at all: `scheduling_foundation` is `20260707064740` in the
ledger and `20260704210155` here, and most of the corpus is the same.

> **NEVER run `supabase db push` against the pilot.** It diffs repo filenames against those versions
> and would try to RE-APPLY nearly every migration in the repo. Apply individually with
> `mcp__supabase-test__apply_migration`, after an `information_schema` audit.

**Drift is RAGGED — never infer a cutoff date.** The audit killed a confident hypothesis:
`host_metrics` (2026-09-01) *was* applied while both of its immediate neighbours were not. There is
no "everything after date D is missing" rule. Diff the whole schema.

**The cheap oracle — schema md5 against the local stack.** The local stack has every migration
applied, so it settles the question in one query run on both sides:

```sql
select md5(string_agg(table_name||'.'||column_name||':'||data_type, ',' order by table_name, column_name)),
       count(*)
  from information_schema.columns where table_schema = 'public';
```

Equal md5 = in sync. Verified equal after the fix: `ff522a3004d8e9ed68dec96de552b732`, 658 columns.

**Fix and proof.** 14 migrations applied in repo order; 20 object-level checks green; first good tick
`2026-09-05 00:31:19Z` — `watchers.tenantErrors: 0`, `teamLeadPlanner planned 1 · decisions 1 ·
applied 1 · recorded 1`, 2 turns / 3 tool calls / 9.7s. No deploy was involved; this was schema-only.

**Auto-deploy is now OFF on both services** (`autoDeploy: no`, `autoDeployTrigger: off` on
`srv-d8uqmdegvqtc73bfn83g` and `crn-d93iq7a8qa3s73ap6260`). **Keep it off.** A merge to `main` must
never be able to ship code to an unmigrated database, and no PR is "too small" to do it.

**One exception to migration-first: a DROP goes code-first.** See the header of
`20260905100000_drop_assignment_locks.sql` — applying a column drop before the deploy breaks the
code still running, which writes it.


### The Opus-5 date-scaffolding verdict — and what one night of evals cost (2026-08-23, overnight)

**User hypothesis confirmed: given ONLY a clock line, Opus 5 made ZERO calendar errors in 91
planner-shaped questions.** Three arms on one anchored corpus (Thursday 2026-08-20): arm B
(seat + full §0f doctrine + steering + resolve_date, data crutches off) 30/31 on real answers;
arm C (seat + clock line only) 26/26 partial; **arm D (tier-2 direct API: clock line + 149 RAW
schedule rows, no doctrine, no resolve_date, no crutches) 73/91 strict with every miss being
something other than a date**: 13 bulk-counting tallies (~5-10% off over 149 rows — every
week/month/quarter BOUNDARY was correct), 1 placed-vs-window semantic, 2 ask-both convention
(removed with the doctrine; it picked a reading AND explained the ambiguity), 2 harness
artifacts (the clock line carried no wall time). It caught the "the 25th is a Friday, right?"
trap cold.

**What survives on merit: server-side counting** — W13's lesson re-proven on a frontier model.
Counting is arithmetic over rows, not date competence, and the planner is structurally immune
only because SQL does its counting (the aggregate-the-summaries rule, generalized: any number
the planner consumes or quotes must be computed by code, never tallied in-context). Everything
else in the GLM-era date stack is, for Opus, replaceable by one sentence. ④b consequence: the
`cleanQuestionDraft` rails stay as reject-only insurance; the case for building MORE code
guards is dead.

**The cost anatomy.** The seat surface costs **~52k input per model call** regardless of arm —
harness prompt + full tool catalog + AGENTS.md; the date scaffolding was ~1-2k of it — at 2-3
calls per question. 186 seat calls = 8.89M input ≈ $45, all full price because **Anthropic's
OpenAI-compat endpoint never caches** (measured cacheRead 0; docs confirm `cache_control` is
native-API, per-request, no console setup; Opus 5 min 512 tokens, reads 0.1x). The tier-2 arm
(`scripts/date-eval/run-direct.mjs`, planner-eval's cached transport) ran the same 91 questions
for **$0.92 in ~4 minutes**. `scripts/planner-eval/anthropic.mjs` caches correctly already;
**production `toolLoop.ts` anthropicTransport sets NO cache_control — live planner ticks run
uncached; a small PR closes a free ~90% input saving.**

**Five traps re-bitten in one night** (all previously recorded): provider balance-out mid-run,
twice (check balance FIRST; empty trajectory + instant dead turn = billing — read the gateway
log); Render env vars are inert until a deploy on an autoDeploy:no service (arm B ran
unanchored once — behavioral verification caught it at question 1); the gateway caches the
spine's tools/list (surface changes need `gateway restart` AFTER the deploy is live); a
surviving `.runid` re-mints the same session ids and replays OLD answers as 1.3s "turns";
and the `-m` newline trap (a two-line clock prefix killed every turn in 1.4s — single line).

Standing eval tooling from the night: `--anchor` + `EVAL_FAKE_NOW` (tenant-scoped anchored
clock — the adversarial regime at any hour; evenings are no longer a scheduling constraint),
`GROUNDING_LEAN` / `GROUNDING_BARE`, `--clock-line`, and the tier-2 direct runner (PR #170).
Fleet fully restored and verified same night: fireworks-prod route, medic active, §0f back on
s2, spine flags inert.

### `openclaw message send` — the ④b transport contract (probed 2026-08-23, s2, dead target)

Docs (docs.openclaw.ai/cli/message) give syntax but omit exit codes, multi-line and hook
behavior; all three probed on-box. **The binary lives IN THE SANDBOX CONTAINER** (no `openclaw`
on the box PATH) — invocation is `nemoclaw <seat> exec --no-tty -- bash -c 'openclaw message
send --channel telegram --target <chatId> -m "$(cat /tmp/<staged>)" --json'`. A multi-line body
MUST go through a staged in-sandbox file exactly like the current runner's `--message-file`
discipline: exec's argv still rejects newlines. **Success signal is deterministic**: exit 1 +
`OutboundDeliveryError: Telegram send failed: chat not found (chat_id=1)` on the dead target;
the gateway logs one `[ws] ⇄ res message.action` line per request (vs `res agent` for the old
relay). **The weekday guard FIRES on this path**: weekday-guard.jsonl grew by one
`{"hook":"message_sending","v":"0.4.0","chars":70,"flags":[]}` record timestamped to the probe
second — direct send keeps full guard coverage, so retiring the relay turn loses nothing.
Channel-history appearance is unobservable on a dead target; that fact lands with ④b Phase 4's
real send.

### Nudge delivery v2 is LIVE — verbatim send, zero inference (④b Phase 4, 2026-08-23)

The box's nudge-runner is **v2** as of 2026-08-23 ~18:40Z (installed md5
`1e4c65b677c33566e93ea344c4689b1d`; service unit comment synced; ALL SIX seats wired with the
new §0k "Questions and items from the office" AGENTS.md block — audited on s2 AND s5, ten
doctrine headings exactly once each). v2 sends the spine's footer-less `directMessageB64`
straight to the person's Telegram with `openclaw message send` (staged in-sandbox file; the
probed transport contract above) and judges by the send's own exit — no agent turn, no
trajectory scraping. **Deploy-order inversion to remember:** the box ran v2 FROM THE FEATURE
BRANCH while `main` still carried v1 until the ④b Phase-4 PR merged — md5-against-repo remains
the only truth about what the box runs, in either direction.

First live E2E (nudge `35ebfd2f`, throwaway subject on Default Tenant): tick 17:55:38Z created
and composed on the same lifecycle run → box tick 17:58:48 sent 147 chars → `asked` at
17:58:49, serve_count 1. Byte-what-arrived proven three independent ways: runner journal
sha256 `8b48506c…f51427` = the DB's own digest of the stored question's pre-footer half = the
weekday guard's `message_sending` record (0 flags) — **guard coverage survived the relay's
retirement**. Delivery leg ~13s vs v1's ~117s agent turn. Duplicate-protection proven live:
row re-queued with the ack suppressed → next tick re-served (serve_count 2), runner hit the
sent-marker under `/var/lib/nudge-runner/`, re-acked with NO second message.

This also answers Stage-1's "STILL UNPINNED" failed-send question for the v2 path: a dead /
never-started chat is a **deterministic exit 1** (`OutboundDeliveryError: chat not found`), so
the runner reports `failed`, the spine keeps serving, and the 10-serve cap hands the gap to
the manager — the silent-by-nature failure mode is now loud row state. (The v1 `--deliver`
behavior on a dead chat remains unmeasured and no longer matters.)

Residue: markers and in-sandbox `/tmp/nudge-<id>.txt` files accumulate (trivial at pilot
scale); marker-before-report covers lost-ack, not a crash between send and `touch` — a
send-then-die re-pings once (at-least-once by design). OPEN at time of writing: whether the
date-eval flags (`GROUNDING_BARE`/`GROUNDING_LEAN`/`EVAL_FAKE_NOW`) are still set on the web
service env — the MCP env write is classifier-blocked, the user owns clearing them; if
`GROUNDING_BARE=1` was captured by the 17:22Z deploy, every seat is on the stripped date
surface until cleared + redeployed (then bounce seat gateways to refresh cached tools/list).
*(Resolved same day: user cleared the three flags, redeploy 18:09Z, s2 gateway bounced 18:17Z.)*

### ④b COMPLETE — and what the briefing probe taught (2026-08-23)

Plan `thoughts/shared/plans/2026-08-23-delivery-costs-nothing.md` is **complete**, all five
phases live-verified in production (PRs #171/#172/#174; note #173 merged into its side branch —
GitHub only retargets a stacked PR when the base branch is deleted at merge — and was re-landed
as #174; check `origin/main` CONTAINS a "merged" PR before believing it landed). Footer retired:
stored = shown = sent (nudge d0050e93 proved the full-hash identity). The seeded world produced
the first fully organic v2 delivery the same hour (Elena Vasquez, threshold crossed at noon).

**The briefing (agentContext) contract, as live-tested:** every mechanical layer works — planner
rail → nudge_decisions → review_items.detail → list_pending_reviews serves it verbatim into the
agent's context. The last inch is ATTENTION, and it behaves exactly as the plan's own note
predicted: a briefed item served inside a ~30-item oldest-first queue was aggregated away
("30+ overdue gaps — dismiss in bulk?"), while an ITEM-FOCUSED question ("what's the deal with
the Marcus Ellery sump pump job?") produced a briefing-informed answer — the briefing's causal
story in the agent's own words, no quoting, no machinery named. **User ruling: the queue-scan
case is a test-design error, not an agent failure — briefings serve item-focused engagement;
there is no salience/economic model that justifies expecting an agent to weight one of 30
undifferentiated items.** Probe design corollary: manager-as-technician makes escalation probes
circular (the ladder is technician-first); the honest rung-2 shortcut specimen is a tech with no
seat/Telegram (hman), whose gap goes straight to the manager queue on the SAME tick — no 48h
plant needed. Also live-observed: ③'s auto-resolve healed a raced escalation on its own
("field report arrived after this was flagged") when the fail-safe (every-5-min SWEEP, not the
half-hourly planner) escalated seconds before the answer landed.

**Follow-ups this surfaced (queue-triage family, unscheduled):** review-queue hygiene/expiry
(~30 stale open items on the pilot from Aug 14-22 — any needle drowns), newest-first or
salience-aware ordering (today: `created_at` ASCENDING, the newest item sorts LAST), bulk-triage
machinery (belongs with the unbuilt pacing/earn-the-ask work), and the briefing-discovery eval
the plan note called for. Rung-2 items are PULL-only today (no proactive manager ping — the
no-unearned-pings doctrine); if managers should be NOTIFIED of escalations, that is pacing-work
scope too.
